Γ ⊢ ( 𝑒 1, 𝑒 2) : 𝜏 1 ∗ 𝜏 2

Fst 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 : 𝜏 1 ∗ 𝜏 2

Γ ⊢ fst 𝑒 : 𝜏 1

Snd 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 : 𝜏 1 ∗ 𝜏 2

Γ ⊢ snd 𝑒 : 𝜏 2

Fun 

Γ, 𝑥 : 𝜏 1 ⊢ 𝑒 : 𝜏 2

Γ ⊢ fun 𝑥 → 𝑒 : 𝜏 1 → 𝜏 2

App 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : 𝜏 1 → 𝜏 2 Γ ⊢ 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 1

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 2

True 

Γ ⊢ true : bool 

False 

Γ ⊢ false : bool 

And 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : bool Γ ⊢ 𝑒 2 : bool 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 && 𝑒 2 : bool 

Or 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : bool Γ ⊢ 𝑒 2 : bool 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 || 𝑒 2 : bool 

Not 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 : bool 

Γ ⊢ not 𝑒 : bool 

Unit 

Γ ⊢ () : unit 

FinConst 

0 ≤ 𝑘 < 𝑛 

Γ ⊢ 𝑘 #𝑛 : fin (𝑛 )

FinLess 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : fin (𝑛 ) Γ ⊢ 𝑒 2 : fin (𝑛 )

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 <#𝑛 𝑒 2 : bool 

FinLessEq 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : fin (𝑛 ) Γ ⊢ 𝑒 2 : fin (𝑛 )

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 ≤#𝑛 𝑒 2 : bool 

Observe 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 : bool 

Γ ⊢ observe 𝑒 : unit 

Seq 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : 𝜏 1 Γ ⊢ 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 2

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1; 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 2

Fix 

Γ, 𝑓 : 𝜏 1 → 𝜏 2, 𝑥 : 𝜏 1 ⊢ 𝑒 : 𝜏 2

Γ ⊢ fix 𝑓 𝑥 := 𝑒 : 𝜏 1 → 𝜏 2

Nil 

Γ ⊢ nil : list (𝜏 )

Cons 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : 𝜏 Γ ⊢ 𝑒 2 : list (𝜏 )

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 :: 𝑒 2 : list (𝜏 )

Match 

Γ ⊢ 𝑒 : list (𝜏 1) Γ ⊢ 𝑒 1 : 𝜏 2 Γ, ℎ : 𝜏 1, 𝑡 : list (𝜏 1) ⊢ 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 2

Γ ⊢ match 𝑒 with nil → 𝑒 1 | ℎ :: 𝑡 → 𝑒 2 end : 𝜏 2

Diverge 

Γ ⊢ diverge : 𝜏

E Discretization Proof E.1 Well-Typedness of Discretization 

Proof of Theorem 5.4. We proceed by induction on the structure of the typing derivation Γ ⊢ 𝑒 : 𝜏 ,matching each case of the discretization function D⟦·⟧ defined in Figure 13. 

Case 𝑒 = 𝑥 .Since Γ(𝑥 ) = 𝜏 , we have D⟦ Γ⟧( 𝑥 ) = D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧. Moreover, D⟦ 𝑥 : 𝜏 ⟧ = 𝑥 , so D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ 𝑥 : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧.

Case 𝑒 = (𝑐 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ]) .The Float-Const rule was applied, so has (𝑉 , 𝑐 ) holds. 

• If 𝐵 = ⊤: then D⟦ 𝑐 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ]⟧ = 𝑐 and D⟦ float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ]⟧ = float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ]. Since has (𝑉 , 𝑐 )

still holds, 𝑐 is well-typed at float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ] by Float-Const. 

• If 𝐵 ≠ ⊤: then D⟦ 𝑐 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ]⟧ = 𝑘 #|𝐵 |+ 1 for the unique 𝑘 such that 𝑐 ∈ Intervals (𝐵 )𝑘 , and 

D⟦ float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ]⟧ = fin (| 𝐵 | + 1). Since 0 ≤ 𝑘 < |𝐵 | + 1, the finite constant 𝑘 #|𝐵 |+ 1 is well-typed at fin (| 𝐵 | + 1) by FinConst. 

Case 𝑒 = (let 𝑥 = 𝑒 1 : 𝜏 1 in 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 2).By the inductive hypothesis, 

D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ D⟦ 𝑒 1 : 𝜏 1⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 1⟧ and D⟦ Γ, 𝑥 : 𝜏 1⟧ ⊢ D⟦ 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 2⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 2⟧.

Note that D⟦ Γ, 𝑥 : 𝜏 1⟧ = D⟦ Γ⟧, 𝑥 : D⟦ 𝜏 1⟧. Applying Let gives 

D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ let 𝑥 = D⟦ 𝑒 1 : 𝜏 1⟧ in D⟦ 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 2⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 2⟧.

Case 𝑒 = (if 𝑒 1 : bool then 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 else 𝑒 3 : 𝜏 ).By the inductive hypothesis, D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ D⟦ 𝑒 𝑖 : 𝜏 𝑖 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 𝑖 ⟧ for 𝑖 = 1, 2, 3, where 𝜏 1 = bool and 

𝜏 2 = 𝜏 3 = 𝜏 . Since D⟦ bool ⟧ = bool , applying If gives 

D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ if D⟦ 𝑒 1 : bool ⟧ then D⟦ 𝑒 2 : 𝜏 ⟧ else D⟦ 𝑒 3 : 𝜏 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧.

Case 𝑒 = (𝑒 1 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 1] < 𝑒 2 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 2]) .The Less rule was applied, so both subexpressions share the same cut set 𝐵 and answers < (𝐵, 𝑉 1, 𝑉 2)

holds. By the inductive hypothesis, D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ D⟦ 𝑒 𝑖 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 𝑖 ]⟧ : D⟦ float [𝐵 ;𝑉 𝑖 ]⟧ for 𝑖 = 1, 2. 

• If 𝐵 = ⊤: then D⟦ float [𝐵 ;𝑉 𝑖 ]⟧ = float [𝐵 ;𝑉 𝑖 ] for both 𝑖 , and the standard float comparison gives type bool = D⟦ bool ⟧.

• If 𝐵 ≠ ⊤: then both subexpressions are discretized to type fin (| 𝐵 | + 1), and the finite comparison 

<#( | 𝐵 |+ 1) is well-typed at bool = D⟦ bool ⟧ by FinLess. 

Case 𝑒 = (𝑒 1 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 1] ≤ 𝑒 2 : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 2]) .Analogous to the < case, using answers ≤ (𝐵, 𝑉 1, 𝑉 2) and FinLessEq. 

Case 𝑒 = (uniform (𝑒 1 : float [𝐵 1;𝑉 1], 𝑒 2 : float [𝐵 2;𝑉 2]) : float [𝐵 ; ⊤]) .The Continuous-2 rule was applied, so recovers (𝐵 1, 𝑉 1) and recovers (𝐵 2, 𝑉 2) hold. By the in-ductive hypothesis, D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ D⟦ 𝑒 𝑖 : float [𝐵 𝑖 ;𝑉 𝑖 ]⟧ : D⟦ float [𝐵 𝑖 ;𝑉 𝑖 ]⟧ for 𝑖 = 1, 2. Also, 𝐵 𝑖 ≡⊤ 𝐵 ,so 𝐵 𝑖 = ⊤ iff 𝐵 = ⊤. We consider each sub-case of the discretization function:

> fin

(| 𝐵 | + 1) by Discrete. 

• If 𝐵, 𝐵 1, 𝐵 2 ≠ ⊤ and 𝑉 1 = ∅ or 𝑉 2 = ∅, then the enumeration over 𝑉 1 × 𝑉 2 is empty, so the generated expression evaluates D⟦ 𝑒 1⟧ and D⟦ 𝑒 2⟧ through its two outer let -bindings and then yields diverge ; the bindings are well-typed by the inductive hypotheses and weakening, and their body is well-typed at fin (| 𝐵 | + 1) by Diverge. 

• 𝐵, 𝐵 1, 𝐵 2 ≠ ⊤, 𝑉 1, 𝑉 2 ≠ ∅, and 𝑒 1 ∉ R or 𝑒 2 ∉ R: the algorithm generates conditionals over all pairs (𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑤 𝑗 ), each branch dispatching to a constant parameter in uniform . The inductive hypotheses and weakening type the two outer let -bindings. In each guard, both operands of each finite equality have the same finite type; since finite equality is desugared using FinLessEq, each equality has type bool , as does their conjunction. By a previous subcase, each branch 

D⟦ uniform (𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑤 𝑗 ) : float [𝐵 ; ⊤]⟧ is well-typed at fin (| 𝐵 | + 1) = D⟦ float [𝐵 ; ⊤]⟧ . The final

branch has the same type by Diverge; hence the conditional structure is well-typed by If (as in the if case above), and the full expression is well-typed at D⟦ float [𝐵 ; ⊤]⟧ .

Case a one-argument continuous distribution. Analogous, using 𝐵 1 ≡⊤ 𝐵 .

Case 𝑒 is any other language construct (functions, pairs, lists, etc. ). By definition of D⟦·⟧ , the discretization recurses structurally over 𝑒 and 𝜏 . That is, D⟦ 𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟧

is formed by applying D⟦·⟧ to each subexpression and subtype of 𝑒 : 𝜏 , and D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧ is formed by applying D⟦·⟧ to each component type of 𝜏 . The result then follows immediately by applying the inductive hypothesis to each subterm, analogous to the above cases. In every case, the typing derivation is preserved under D⟦·⟧ . Therefore, 

D⟦ Γ⟧ ⊢ D⟦ 𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧. □

F Soundness Proof F.1 Measure Theory Preliminaries 

We first give a brief overview of basic measure-theoretic terms and lemmas, and refer the reader to standard textbooks for further background [43, 44]. 

Topological space. A topological space is a pair (𝑋, T ) where T is a collection of subsets of 𝑋 ,called open sets, such that ∅, 𝑋 ∈ T , and T is closed under arbitrary unions and finite intersections. 

𝜎 -algebra. A 𝜎 -algebra on a set 𝑋 is a collection Σ of subsets of 𝑋 that includes 𝑋 itself and is closed under complement and under countable unions. By implication, a 𝜎 -algebra includes the empty set ∅ and is closed under countable intersections. Given a topological space (𝑋, T ) , the Borel 

𝜎 -algebra B( 𝑋 ) is the smallest 𝜎 -algebra containing all open sets in T . 

> Proc. ACM Program. Lang., Vol. 10, No. OOPSLA2, Article 402. Publication date: October 2026. Type-Directed Discretization of Probabilistic Programs (Extended Version) 402:43

Measurable space. A measurable space is a pair (𝑋, Σ) consisting of a set 𝑋 and a 𝜎 -algebra Σ on 

𝑋 . When Σ is the Borel 𝜎 -algebra on a topological space 𝑋 , we call (𝑋, Σ) a Borel measurable space. 

Measure. Let (𝑋, Σ) be a measurable space. A function 𝜇 : Σ → [ 0, ∞] is called a measure if it satisfies the following properties: nonnegativity, 𝜇 (∅) = 0, and countable additivity. When Σ is a Borel 𝜎 -algebra, 𝜇 is called a Borel measure. 

Measure space. The triple (𝑋, Σ, 𝜇 ) is called a measure space. When Σ is the Borel 𝜎 -algebra and 

𝜇 is a Borel measure, (𝑋, Σ, 𝜇 ) is called a Borel measure space. 

Subprobability measure. Let (𝑋, Σ, 𝜇 ) be a measure space. When 𝜇 (𝑋 ) ≤ 1, 𝜇 is called a (Borel) subprobability measure. When 𝜇 (𝑋 ) = 1, 𝜇 is called a (Borel) probability measure and (𝑋, Σ, 𝜇 ) a(Borel) probability space. 

Product measurable space. The product of two measurable spaces (𝑋 1, Σ1) and (𝑋 2, Σ2) is also a measurable space, namely (𝑋 1 × 𝑋 2, Σ1 × Σ2), where Σ1 × Σ2 is the 𝜎 -algebra generated by sets of the form 𝐴 1 × 𝐴 2 with 𝐴 1 ∈ Σ1 and 𝐴 2 ∈ Σ2.

Generated 𝜎 -algebra. Let 𝑋 be a set and let G ⊆ P ( 𝑋 ) be a collection of subsets of 𝑋 . The 𝜎 -algebra generated by G, denoted 𝜎 (G) , is the smallest 𝜎 -algebra on 𝑋 that contains G. Equivalently, it is the intersection of all 𝜎 -algebras on 𝑋 that contain G.

Subprobability distribution. Let (𝑋, Σ) be a measurable space. We write D ( 𝑋 ) for the set of all subprobability measures on (𝑋, Σ).

Dirac measure. Let (𝑋, Σ) be a measurable space and let 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 . The Dirac measure at 𝑥 is the probability measure 

𝛿 𝑥 : Σ → [ 0, 1]

defined by 

𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 ) =

(

1 if 𝑥 ∈ 𝐴 

0 if 𝑥 ∉ 𝐴. 

Measurable function. A function 𝑓 : 𝑋 → 𝑌 between measurable spaces (𝑋, F𝑋 ) and (𝑌, F𝑌 ) is called measurable if the preimage of any measurable set in 𝑌 is measurable in 𝑋 , i.e. if 

𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ) = {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑓 (𝑥 ) ∈ 𝐴 } ∈ F 𝑋 

for all 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑌 .

Definition F.1 (Subspace 𝜎 -algebra). Let (𝑋, Σ) be a Borel measurable space and let 𝑌 ⊆ 𝑋 . The subspace (or trace) 𝜎 -algebra on 𝑌 is defined as 

Σ𝑌 = {𝑌 ∩ 𝐴 | 𝐴 ∈ Σ}.

Then (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ) is a Borel measurable space. 

Definition F.2 ( 𝜎 -algebra generated by a function). Let 𝑓 : 𝑋 → 𝑌 be a function and let (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ) be a measurable space. The 𝜎 -algebra generated by 𝑓 is defined by: 

𝑓 −1 (Σ𝑌 ) := {𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ) | 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 }.

Then (𝑋, 𝑓 −1 (Σ𝑌 )) is a measurable space.  

> Proc. ACM Program. Lang., Vol. 10, No. OOPSLA2, Article 402. Publication date: October 2026. 402:44 Katherine Wu, Jules Jacobs, Kevin Batz, and Alexandra Silva

Lemma F.3 (Bijection induces isomorphism). Let 𝑓 : 𝑋 → 𝑌 be a bijection and let (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ) be a measurable space. Equip 𝑋 with the 𝜎 -algebra generated by 𝑓 :

Σ𝑋 := 𝑓 −1 (Σ𝑌 ) = {𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ) | 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 }.

Then, (1) (𝑋, Σ𝑋 ) is a measurable space, and (2) 𝑓 induces an isomorphism of measurable spaces: 

(𝑋, Σ𝑋 )  (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ).

Proof. We first verify that i) (𝑋, Σ𝑋 ) is a measurable space, then show that ii) 𝑓 induces an isomorphism of measurable spaces (𝑋, Σ𝑋 )  (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ).

Proof of i) We show that Σ𝑋 = 𝑓 −1 (Σ𝑌 ) is a 𝜎 -algebra on 𝑋 .

• (Contains 𝑋 ): 𝑋 = 𝑓 −1 (𝑌 ) ∈ Σ𝑋 since 𝑌 ∈ Σ𝑌 .

• (Complements): If 𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ) ∈ Σ𝑋 , then 𝑋 \ 𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ) = 𝑓 −1 (𝑌 \ 𝐴 ) ∈ Σ𝑋 since 𝑌 \ 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 .

• (Countable unions): If 𝑓 −1 (𝐴 𝑖 ) ∈ Σ𝑋 for 𝑖 ∈ N, then Ð𝑖 𝑓 −1 (𝐴 𝑖 ) = 𝑓 −1 (Ð𝑖 𝐴 𝑖 ) ∈ Σ𝑋 since 

Ð𝑖 𝐴 𝑖 ∈ Σ𝑌 .Hence (𝑋, Σ𝑋 ) is a measurable space. 

Proof of ii) Two measurable spaces (𝑋, Σ𝑋 ) and (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ) are isomorphic iff there exists a bijection 

𝑓 : 𝑋 → 𝑌 such that 𝑓 and 𝑓 −1 are measurable. We first show that 𝑓 is measurable. Measurability of 𝑓 means that 𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ) ∈ Σ𝑋 for every 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 .Let 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 be arbitrary. By definition of Σ𝑋 ,

𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ) ∈ 𝑓 −1 (Σ𝑌 ) = Σ𝑋 .

It follows that 𝑓 is measurable. Next we show that 𝑓 −1 is measurable. Measurability of 𝑓 −1 means that (𝑓 −1)−1 (𝐵 ) ∈ Σ𝑌 for every 𝐵 ∈ Σ𝑋 . Let 𝐵 ∈ Σ𝑋 be arbitrary. By definition of Σ𝑋 , there exists some 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 such that 

𝐵 = 𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ).Observe that: 

(𝑓 −1)−1 (𝐵 ) = 𝑓 (𝐵 ) (preimage of 𝑓 −1 is 𝑓 )

= 𝑓 (𝑓 −1 (𝐴 )) (substituting 𝐵 = 𝑓 −1 (𝐴 ))

= 𝐴 (𝑓 is a bijection) Since 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 , it follows that 𝑓 −1 is measurable. Therefore 𝑓 is a measurable bijection with measurable inverse, so it is an isomorphism of measurable spaces. □

is a 𝜎 -algebra on 𝑋 such that G ⊆ C . Then 

𝜎 (G) ⊆ C .

Definition F.5 (Sub-Markov kernels). Given measurable spaces (𝑋, Σ𝑋 ) and (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ), a sub-Markov kernel from 𝑋 to 𝑌 is a function 

𝑃 : 𝑋 × Σ𝑌 → [ 0, 1]

such that: (1) for every 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 , 𝑃 (𝑥, −) is a subprobability measure on 𝑌 ;(2) for every 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑌 , the map 𝑥 ↦ → 𝑃 (𝑥, 𝐴 ) is measurable. A Markov kernel is a sub-Markov kernel satisfying 𝑃 (𝑥, 𝑌 ) = 1 for every 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 .

Definition F.6 (Projection with error). Let (𝑋 + ⊥ , Σ𝑋 +⊥ ) and (𝑌 + ⊥ , Σ𝑌 +⊥ ) be measurable spaces, with (𝑋, Σ𝑋 ) and (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ) measurable spaces as well. Let 𝜇 ∈ D (( 𝑋 × 𝑌 ) + ⊥) . We define the first and second projection measures as: (1) 𝜋 1 (𝜇 )( 𝐴 ) := 𝜇 (𝐴 × 𝑌 ) for every measurable 𝐴 ∈ Σ𝑋 , and 𝜋 1 (𝜇 )(⊥) := 𝜇 (⊥) 

(2) 𝜋 2 (𝜇 )( 𝐵 ) := 𝜇 (𝑋 × 𝐵 ) for every measurable 𝐵 ∈ Σ𝑌 , and 𝜋 2 (𝜇 )(⊥) := 𝜇 (⊥) 

Then, 𝜋 1 (𝜇 ) is a measure on (𝑋 + ⊥ , Σ𝑋 +⊥ ) and 𝜋 2 (𝜇 ) is a measure on (𝑌 + ⊥ , Σ𝑌 +⊥ ).

F.2 Measure Space on Expressions 

To make the probabilistic semantics precise, we construct an appropriate measure space structure on the set of expressions by factoring out real numbers using the notion of what we call expression skeletons .

Definition F.7 (Expression Skeleton). An expression skeleton 𝑠 is a syntactic expression obtained from a program by replacing each occurrence of a float constant with a hole of the form □. The set of skeletons is generated by the grammar: 

𝑠 :: = □ | 𝑥 | true | false | 𝑘 #𝑛 | diverge | () | let 𝑥 = 𝑠 1 in 𝑠 2 | if 𝑠 1 then 𝑠 2 else 𝑠 3

| 𝑠 1 < 𝑠 2 | 𝑠 1 ≤ 𝑠 2 | 𝑠 1 <#𝑛 𝑠 2 | 𝑠 1 ≤#𝑛 𝑠 2

We denote by 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 the set of all expression skeletons, and by ℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) the number of distinct holes in skeleton 𝑠 .

Example F.8. The expression uniform (0.0, 1.0) < 0.5 has the skeleton uniform (□, □) < □ with three holes. 

Definition F.9 (Filling a skeleton). Let 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 and let ®𝑟 = (𝑟 1, . . . , 𝑟 ℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) ) ∈ Rℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) . Define the expression 𝑠 [® 𝑟 ] ∈ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 to be the result of replacing each hole □𝑖 in 𝑠 with the corresponding real constant 𝑟 𝑖 , for 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ ℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ).To recover a typed notion of skeletons, we extend the typing judgment to skeletons by adding a distinguished typing rule for holes.  

> Proc. ACM Program. Lang., Vol. 10, No. OOPSLA2, Article 402. Publication date: October 2026. 402:46 Katherine Wu, Jules Jacobs, Kevin Batz, and Alexandra Silva

Definition F.10 (Well-typed expression skeleton). Define a typing judgment for skeletons, written 

Γ ⊢ 𝑠 : 𝜏 , with the understanding that all typing rules from expressions are preserved for skeletons, and that holes are typed as float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ] according to the rule below: 

□ : float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ]

We denote by 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 the set of all well-typed expression skeletons, where: 

𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 := {𝑠 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 | 𝑠 : 𝜏 }.

Remark F.11 . Both 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 and 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 are to be understood as the set of all well-typed skeletons and expressions, respectively, equipped with their full subtype information .For example, 

□ : float [𝐵 ; {0.5}] < □ : float [𝐵 ; {0.5}] and □ : float [𝐵 ; {0.2}] < □ : float [𝐵 ; {0.8}] 

are distinct elements of 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 bool , while 0.5 : float [𝐵 ; {0.5}] < 0.5 : float [𝐵 ; {0.5}] and 0.2 : float [𝐵 ; {0.2}] < 0.8 : float [𝐵 ; {0.8}] 

are distinct elements of 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 bool .In particular, both 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 and 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 are in general uncountable, since float subtype annotations of the form float [𝐵 ;𝑉 ] may vary over uncountably many real values. 

Definition F.12 (Decomposing an expression). The decomposition function 

dec : 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 −→

{𝑠 } × Rℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) 

is defined as the inverse of filling a skeleton, i.e., for every expression 𝑒 : 𝜏 ,

dec (𝑒 ) = (𝑠, ®𝑟 ) such that 𝑒 = 𝑠 [® 𝑟 ].

Definition F.13 (Hole assignments of a skeleton). For a given 𝑠 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 , define the set of hole assignments of 𝑠 as: 

𝐴 𝑠 :𝜏 :=

Ø 

> 𝑒 :𝜏 ∈𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏

{dec (𝑒 : 𝜏 )2 | dec (𝑒 : 𝜏 )1 = 𝑠 : 𝜏 } ⊆ Rℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 )

Intuitively, 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 is the set of all real tuples that can be substituted into the holes of 𝑠 to yield a well-typed expression of type 𝜏 .

Example F.14. Consider the skeleton 

𝑠 : 𝜏 = (□ : float [{ <3.5, <4.5}; ⊤] < □ : float [{ <3.5, <4.5}; {3.5, 4.5}]) : bool .

This skeleton has two holes: 

• The left hole has type float [{ <3.5, <4.5}; ⊤] , so it may be instantiated by any real number. 

• The right hole has type float [{ <3.5, <4.5}; {3.5, 4.5}] , so it may only be instantiated by one of the values 3 .5 or 4 .5. An expression 𝑒 : bool has skeleton 𝑠 iff it is of the form 

𝑒 = 𝑐 1 < 𝑐 2

where 𝑐 1 ∈ R and 𝑐 2 ∈ { 3.5, 4.5}.

Definition F.15 ( 𝜎 -algebra construction on 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 ). Let 𝑠 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 . Endow 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 with the subspace

Lemma F.16. (𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 , F𝑠 ;𝜏 ) is a Borel measurable space. 

Proof. For a fixed 𝑠 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 , (Rℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) , B( Rℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) )) is a Borel measurable space and 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 ⊆

Rℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) . Thus, by Definition F.1, (𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 , {𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 ∩ 𝑀 | 𝑀 ∈ B( Rℎ𝑜𝑙𝑒𝑠 (𝑠 ) )}) = (𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 , F𝑠 ;𝜏 ) is a Borel measurable space. □

Now, we map this measurable space to the space of actual expressions that share that skeleton.

Definition F.17 (Expressions of a skeleton). For a given 𝑠 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 , define the set of well-typed expressions having skeleton 𝑠 as: 

𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 := { 𝑒 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 | dec (𝑒 : 𝜏 )1 = 𝑠 } ⊆ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 .

Intuitively, 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 consists exactly of those expressions whose syntactic shape is fixed by 𝑠 , and whose real constants vary according to the admissible hole assignments in 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 .

Example F.18. Consider the skeleton 

𝑠 : 𝜏 = (□ : float [{ <3.5, <4.5}; ⊤] < □ : float [{ <3.5, <4.5}; {3.5, 4.5}]) : bool .

An expression 𝑒 : bool belongs to 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 iff it is of the form 

𝑒 = 𝑐 1 < 𝑐 2

where 𝑐 1 ∈ R and 𝑐 2 ∈ { 3.5, 4.5}.Therefore, 

𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 = {𝑐 1 < 𝑐 2 | 𝑐 1 ∈ R, 𝑐 2 ∈ { 3.5, 4.5}} .

Definition F.19 ( 𝜎 -algebra construction on 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 ). Let 𝑠 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 . Endow 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 with the following 𝜎 -algebra: 

F𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 := {𝑒 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 | dec (𝑒 : 𝜏 )2 ∈ 𝐵 } | 𝐵 ∈ F 𝑠 ;𝜏 .

Lemma F.20. For a fixed 𝑠 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 , the map 

dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 : 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 → 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 , 𝑒 ↦ → dec (𝑒 )2.

is a bijection. 

Proof. Let 𝑠 : 𝜏 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 .

(i) Injectivity: Suppose 𝑒 1, 𝑒 2 ∈ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 satisfy dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 (𝑒 1) = dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 (𝑒 2). By definition of dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 ,

dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 (𝑒 1) = dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 (𝑒 2) = ®𝑟 for some ®𝑟 ∈ 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 . Since both 𝑒 1 and 𝑒 2 have skeleton 𝑠 by assumption, we have 𝑒 1 = 𝑠 [® 𝑟 ] and 𝑒 2 = 𝑠 [® 𝑟 ], hence 𝑒 1 = 𝑒 2.

(ii) Surjectivity: Let ®𝑟 ∈ 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 . By definition of 𝐴 𝑠 ;𝜏 , there exists some expression 𝑒 ∈ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 such that dec (𝑒 ) = (𝑠, ®𝑟 ). Then 𝑒 ∈ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 by construction of 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 , and dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 (𝑒 ) = ®𝑟 .

dec 𝑠 ;𝜏 is injective and surjective, hence a bijection. □

Now we combine the measurable spaces for each distinct skeleton into a single measurable space of all well-typed expressions of type 𝜏 . The set of all well-typed expressions of type 𝜏 is thus the disjoint union:

Proof. We prove the measurability of 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 , {⊥} , and 𝑁 𝜏 in separate parts. For each, we show that it belongs to the 𝜎 -algebra F𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 +⊥ on the extended expression space 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥ .(1) (Measurability of 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 ): Every expression skeleton 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 is either a value skeleton or a non-value skeleton, depending on whether the syntactic form of 𝑠 is a value. An expression 

𝑒 ∈ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 is a value iff its skeleton is a value skeleton. To show 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 ∈ F 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 , by Definition F.23, it suffices to show that ∀𝑠 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 , 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 ∩

𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 ∈ F 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 .Fix 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 :

• If 𝑠 is a value skeleton, then every expression in 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 is a value, so 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 ∩ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 =

• If 𝑠 is a non-value skeleton, then no expression in 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 is a value, so 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 ∩ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 =

∅ ∈ F 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 .Thus, 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 ∩ 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 ∈ F 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝑠 ;𝜏 for any 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆𝑘𝑒𝑙 𝜏 , so 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 ∈ F 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 ⊆ F 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 +⊥ .(2) (Measurability of {⊥} ): Taking 𝐴 = ∅ in Definition F.25 gives {⊥} ∈ F 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 +⊥ .(3) (Measurability of 𝑁 𝜏 ): 𝑁 𝜏 = 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 \ 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 is the complement of 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 in 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 . □

Note that in general, given (𝑋, Σ𝑋 ) and (𝑌, Σ𝑌 ) to be measurable spaces, (( 𝑋 × 𝑌 ) + ⊥) forms a measurable space, since the product (𝑋 × 𝑌 ) is measurable and for any measurable space (𝑍, Σ𝑍 ),the space (𝑍 + ⊥ , Σ𝑍 +⊥ ) with Σ𝑍 +⊥ generated by Σ𝑍 ∪ ⊥ is measurable.

• If 𝑒 = ⊥, then 𝑓 ⊥ (⊥) = 𝛿 ⊥, the Dirac measure at ⊥, which is a subprobability measure on 

𝑌 + ⊥ .

• If 𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 , then 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 ) = 𝑓 (𝑒 ), and since 𝑓 is a sub-Markov kernel by assumption, 𝑓 (𝑒 ) is a subprobability measure on 𝑌 + ⊥ .Thus, for all 𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ , the map 𝐴 ↦ → 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ) is a subprobability measure. 

(2) Measurable function: We show for each 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑌 +⊥ , the map 𝑒 ↦ → 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ) is a measurable function. Fix 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑌 +⊥ . Define 

𝑔 : (𝑋 + ⊥) → [ 0, 1], 𝑔 (𝑒 ) := 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ).

We prove 𝑔 is measurable by showing 𝑔 −1 (𝐵 ) ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ for every 𝐵 ∈ B([ 0, 1]) . It suffices to prove that for every 𝑎 ∈ R,

𝑔 −1 (( 𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1]) ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ 

because the family of sets {( 𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1] : 𝑎 ∈ R} generates B([ 0, 1]) .Because 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1] for all 𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ by definition, we have for every 𝑎 ∈ R,

𝑔 −1  (𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1] = {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ ( 𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1]} 

= {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ ( 𝑎, ∞)) ∧ ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1])} 

= {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 ) ∧ ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1])} 

= {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 },

where the last step uses that 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1] always holds. Hence it suffices to show that {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 

𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 } ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ for every 𝑎 ∈ R.

• If 𝑎 ≥ 1: {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 } = ∅ ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ .

• If 𝑎 < 0: {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 } = 𝑋 + ⊥ ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ .

• If 0 ≤ 𝑎 < 1: {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 }.The only nontrivial case is the last. It splits into two sets: 

{𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 } ={𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ | 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ) > 𝑎 }

={𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ) > 𝑎 } ∪ {⊥ | 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ) > 𝑎 }

={𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑓 (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ) > 𝑎 } ∪ {⊥ | 𝛿 ⊥ (𝐴 ) > 𝑎 }

The first set is measurable in 𝑋 (and hence 𝑋 + ⊥ ) since 𝑓 is a sub-Markov kernel by assumption. The second set is either ∅ ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ or {⊥} ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ . Since F𝑋 +⊥ is closed under countable unions, then 𝑔 −1 (𝐵 ) ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ for every 𝐵 ∈ B([ 0, 1]) . Thus, for all 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑌 +⊥ , the map 𝑒 ↦ → 𝑓 ⊥ (𝑒 )( 𝐴 ) is a measurable function. Since both conditions hold, 𝑓 ⊥ is a sub-Markov kernel. □

Lemma F.31 (Dirac return is a sub-Markov kernel). For any measurable space (𝑋, F𝑋 ), the map 

return : 𝑋 → D ( 𝑋 + ⊥) , 𝑥 ↦ → 𝛿 𝑥 .

is a sub-Markov kernel. 

Proof. We verify the two conditions for return to be a sub-Markov kernel.  

> Proc. ACM Program. Lang., Vol. 10, No. OOPSLA2, Article 402. Publication date: October 2026. 402:54 Katherine Wu, Jules Jacobs, Kevin Batz, and Alexandra Silva

(1) Subprobability measure: We show for each 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 , the map 

𝐴 ↦ → return (𝑥 )( 𝐴 ) = 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 )

is a subprobability measure. Fix 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 . 𝛿 𝑥 is the Dirac measure at 𝑥 , hence a probability measure and therefore a subprobability measure on 𝑋 + ⊥ . Thus, for every 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 , the map 𝐴 ↦ → 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 ) is a subprobability measure. 

(2) Measurable function: We show that for each 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ , the map 

𝑥 ↦ → return (𝑥 )( 𝐴 ) = 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 )

is a measurable function. Fix 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ . Define 

𝑔 : 𝑋 → [ 0, 1], 𝑔 (𝑥 ) := 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 ).

We prove 𝑔 is measurable by showing 𝑔 −1 (𝐵 ) ∈ F 𝑋 for every 𝐵 ∈ B([ 0, 1]) . It suffices to prove that for every 𝑎 ∈ R,

𝑔 −1  (𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1] ∈ F 𝑋 ,

because the family {( 𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1] : 𝑎 ∈ R} generates B([ 0, 1]) .Because 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1] for all 𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 + ⊥ , we have for every 𝑎 ∈ R,

𝑔 −1  (𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1] = {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ ( 𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1]} 

= {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 | ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ ( 𝑎, ∞)) ∧ ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1])} 

= {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 | ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 ) ∧ ( 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1])} 

= {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 },

where the last step uses that 𝑔 (𝑒 ) ∈ [ 0, 1] always holds. Hence it suffices to show that {𝑒 ∈ 𝑋 |

𝑔 (𝑒 ) > 𝑎 } ∈ F 𝑋 for every 𝑎 ∈ R.

• If 𝑎 ≥ 1: {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑔 (𝑥 ) > 𝑎 } = ∅ ∈ F 𝑋 .

• If 𝑎 < 0: {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑔 (𝑥 ) > 𝑎 } = 𝑋 ∈ F 𝑋 .

• If 0 ≤ 𝑎 < 1: {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑔 (𝑥 ) > 𝑎 }.The only nontrivial case is the last. Using 𝑔 (𝑥 ) = 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 ) and the fact that 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 ) ∈ { 0, 1} for all 

𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 , we have for 0 ≤ 𝑎 < 1: 

{𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑔 (𝑥 ) > 𝑎 } = {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 ) > 𝑎 }

= {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝛿 𝑥 (𝐴 ) = 1}

= {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑥 ∈ 𝐴 }

= 𝐴 ∩ 𝑋 . 

Since 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ and F𝑋 = {𝑋 ∩ 𝐴 | 𝐴 ∈ F 𝑋 +⊥ } is the subspace 𝜎 -algebra on 𝑋 ⊆ 𝑋 + ⊥ , it follows that 𝐴 ∩ 𝑋 ∈ F 𝑋 .Therefore {𝑥 ∈ 𝑋 | 𝑔 (𝑥 ) > 𝑎 } ∈ F 𝑋 for all 𝑎 ∈ R, so 𝑔 −1  (𝑎, ∞) ∩ [ 0, 1] ∈ F 𝑋 for all 𝑎 . Hence 

𝑔 −1 (𝐵 ) ∈ F 𝑋 for every 𝐵 ∈ B([ 0, 1]) , and 𝑔 is measurable. □

F.4 Small-Step Semantics 

Throughout this section, we denote by 𝑒 : 𝜏 a well-typed expression of type 𝜏 and implicitly assume that all sub-expressions are annotated with types, as well. Moreover, for the typed expression 

𝑒 : 𝜏 = uniform (𝑒 1 : float [𝐵 1;𝑉 1], 𝑒 2 : float [𝐵 2;𝑉 2]) : float [𝐵, 𝑉 ] with 𝐵, 𝐵 1, 𝐵 2 ≠ ⊤ and 

𝑉 1, 𝑉 2 ≠ ⊤, we introduce an auxiliary statement DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 and let 

D⟦ 𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟧ = DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 (D⟦ 𝑒 1 : float [𝐵 1;𝑉 1]⟧ , D⟦ 𝑒 2 : float [𝐵 2;𝑉 2]⟧) .

We define the semantics of DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 in such a way that it behaves like the discretized program in the last case of Figure 13 but resolves the let-bindings and the conditional choices in a single step, which will simplify our proof. We equip this statement with the following typing rule : 

TDiscreteCase 

𝑒 1 : fin (| 𝐵 1 | + 1) 𝑒 2 : fin (| 𝐵 2 | + 1) recovers (𝐵 1, 𝑉 1) recovers (𝐵 2, 𝑉 2) 

> DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2

(𝑒 1, 𝑒 2) : fin (| 𝐵 | + 1)

Equipped with these notions, we define the small-step semantics of our language:

Proof. The first claim follows from Definition F.1. The second follows from the same error-extension construction used for 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥ . □

As a sanity check, we show that the 𝑛 -step semantics defines a sub-Markov kernel. Lemma F.47 ( 𝑛 -step semantics is a sub-Markov kernel). For all 𝑛 ∈ N, ⟨·⟩ 𝑛 : 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 → D ( 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 +⊥) is a sub-Markov kernel. 

Proof. By induction on 𝑛 .Base case: 𝑛 = 0. By definition, 

⟨𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟩𝑛 = 𝛿 𝑒 :𝜏 

i.e. the map 𝑒 ↦ → 𝛿 𝑒 . By Lemma F.31, this is a sub-Markov kernel. Inductive step: 𝑛 > 0. By definition, 

⟨𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟩𝑛 = ⟨𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟩𝑛 −1 ≫= 𝜆𝑒 ′.⟦𝑒 ′ : 𝜏 ⟧

By the I.H., ⟨·⟩ 𝑛 −1 : 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 → D ( 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥) is a sub-Markov kernel. By Lemma F.39, 𝜆𝑒 ′.⟦𝑒 ′ : 𝜏 ⟧ :

𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 → D ( 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥) is a sub-Markov kernel. By Lemma F.30, its ⊥-extension from (𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥) 

to D ( 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥) is also a sub-Markov kernel. By Lemma F.33, ⟨𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟩𝑛 −1 ≫= 𝜆𝑒 ′.⟦𝑒 ′ : 𝜏 ⟧ is a sub-Markov kernel. □

Proof. Analogous to proof of Lemma F.47. □

is also measurable in 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥ .

We equip 𝑉 𝜏 + ⊥ with the subspace 𝜎 -algebra inherited from 𝑃 𝜏 + ⊥ .Lemma F.51 (Monotonicity of n-step coupling semantics). For every (𝑒 : 𝜏, D⟦ 𝑒 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧) ∈ 𝑃 𝜏 ,

Proof. The coupled small-step semantics maps every element of 𝑉 𝜏 to its Dirac measure, while the 

⊥-extension maps ⊥ to 𝛿 ⊥. Thus every element of 𝑉 𝜏 + ⊥ is absorbing. The remainder is analogous to the proof of Lemma F.49, replacing 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥ by 𝑃 𝜏 + ⊥ and 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 + ⊥ by 𝑉 𝜏 + ⊥ . □

Lemma F.52. For every (𝑒 : 𝜏, D⟦ 𝑒 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧) ∈ 𝑃 𝜏 ,

That is, the coupled big-step semantics is a subprobability measure on 𝑉 𝜏 + ⊥ .

Proof. Analogous to the proof of Lemma F.50, replacing 𝐸𝑥𝑝𝑟 𝜏 + ⊥ by 𝑃 𝜏 + ⊥ , 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 + ⊥ by 𝑉 𝜏 + ⊥ ,and using Lemma F.51 for monotonicity. □

Lemma F.55 ( 𝑛 -step coupling projection). For all 𝑛 ∈ N,(1) 𝜋 1 (⟨ 𝑒 : 𝜏, D⟦ 𝑒 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧⟩ 𝑛 ) = ⟨𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟩𝑛 

(2) 𝜋 2 (⟨ 𝑒 : 𝜏, D⟦ 𝑒 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧⟩ 𝑛 ) = ⟨D⟦ 𝑒 ⟧ : D⟦ 𝜏 ⟧⟩ 𝑛 

Proof. By induction on 𝑛 . We prove i), as the proof for ii) is analogous. Base case: 𝑛 = 0

= 𝜋 1 (𝛿 (𝑒 :𝜏, D⟦ 𝑒 ′⟧:D⟦ 𝜏 ′⟧) ) (definition of 𝑛 -step coupling semantics) 

= 𝛿 𝑒 :𝜏 (projection of Dirac) 

= ⟨𝑒 : 𝜏 ⟩0. (definition of 𝑛 -step semantics)

Before concluding the soundness proof, we relate the auxiliary statement DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 used in the coupling semantics to the nested target expression generated by the discretization function in Figure 13. Lemma F.56. Suppose an expression 𝐹 (𝑒 1, 𝑒 2) evaluates 𝑒 1 first, then 𝑒 2, and, when both arguments are values 𝑣 1, 𝑣 2, continues as 𝑞 (𝑣 1, 𝑣 2). Then 

⟨𝐹 (𝑒 1, 𝑒 2)⟩ = ⟨𝑒 1⟩ ≫ = 𝜆𝑣 1.⟨𝑒 2⟩ ≫ = 𝜆𝑣 2.⟨𝑞 (𝑣 1, 𝑣 2)⟩ .

All functions bound by ≫= are extended to ⊥ as in Definition F.29. 

Proof. By induction on the 𝑛 -step semantics, using the small-step rules for evaluating the first and second arguments and associativity of ≫=. Taking the limit as 𝑛 → ∞ gives the result. □

Theorem F.57 ( DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 is a valid construct). Let 

𝑒 = uniform (𝑒 1 : float [𝐵 1;𝑉 1], 𝑒 2 : float [𝐵 2;𝑉 2]) : float [𝐵 ; ⊤] 

be well typed, where 𝐵, 𝐵 1, 𝐵 2 ≠ ⊤ and 𝑉 1, 𝑉 2 ≠ ⊤. Let 𝑒 case be the nested let - and if -expression in the last case of Figure 13. Then 

⟨DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 (D⟦ 𝑒 1 : float [𝐵 1;𝑉 1]⟧ , D⟦ 𝑒 2 : float [𝐵 2;𝑉 2]⟧) : fin (| 𝐵 |+ 1)⟩ = ⟨𝑒 case : fin (| 𝐵 |+ 1)⟩ .

𝑞 (𝑘 1, 𝑘 2) =

if there exists (𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑤 𝑗 ) ∈ 𝑉 1 × 𝑉 2 such that

> diverge

if no such pair exists .

By the operational rule for DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 and Lemma F.56,

⟨DC 𝐵,𝐵 1,𝐵 2,𝑉 1,𝑉 2 (𝑑 1, 𝑑 2)⟩ = ⟨𝑒 case ⟩. □

Theorem F.59. Let 𝑒 : bool . Then ⟨𝑒 : bool ⟩ = ⟨D⟦ 𝑒 : bool ⟧ : bool ⟩.

Proof. Since D ( 𝑉 𝑎𝑙𝑠 𝜏 + ⊥) contains only discrete distributions, we proceed by case distinction on the probability of the final outcome 𝑣 ∈ { true , false , ⊥} .

The case 𝑣 = true . We have

G Benchmarks G.1 Asymptotic Scaling 

G.1.1 Code Sketches for Scaling Benchmarks. This section contains code snippets of the programs (Figures 29a to 29d and 30a to 30d) used in the scaling experiments in §7.2. We implemented a Python script that automatically generates these programs based on configurable parameters such as program size, guard structure, and dependency pattern. For reproducibility, we used a fixed random seed to generate each program. All 𝑟 𝑖 values in the generated programs, where 0 ≤ 𝑟 𝑖 ≤ 1is randomly chosen, are rounded to two decimal places. All of these programs follow the same structural pattern: they define a sequence of random variables 𝑋 1, . . . , 𝑋 𝑛 , where each 𝑋 𝑖 is drawn from a uniform distribution whose parameters depend on the value of a single predecessor variable through a conditional branch of the form: 

𝑋 𝑖 =

(Unif (𝑎 𝑖 , 𝑏 𝑖 ), 𝑋 𝜋 (𝑖 ) < 𝑟 𝑖 ,

Unif (𝑎 ′ 

> 𝑖

, 𝑏 ′ 

> 𝑖

), otherwise ,

for some parent selection function 𝜋 : {2, . . . , 𝑛 } → { 1, . . . , 𝑖 − 1}. Thus each program is a Bayesian network of conditionally independent nodes, differing only in the choice of 𝜋 (𝑖 ) that determines the dependency pattern. The code snippets show representative size 5 instances from each benchmark family. The scaling experiments generate larger instances of the same form by varying the program size and, for the alternating benchmarks, the guard span. The programs in Figure 29a follow the scheme depicted above where the program size is 𝑛 , i.e., the number of comparisons performed is 𝑛 . In Figure 29b, each 𝑥 𝑖 does not necessarily depend on 𝑥 𝑖 −1 but on some randomly chosen predecessor 3.In Figures 29c and 29d, we additionally choose at random whether the guards of the conditionals and the branches are either some predecessor variable 𝑥 𝑖 or a uniform -sampling instruction. The programs in Figure 30a also follow the scheme depicted above but where the variables used in the guards cycle with a fixed span size of 𝑚 . For instance, for 𝑚 = 2, the pattern is 𝑥 1, 𝑥 2, 𝑥 1, . . . . The programs in Figures 30b to 30d follow a similar pattern. We report runtime (seconds) for (i) Sppl, (ii) Slice (discretization only), (iii) Slice+Dice (dis-cretization + inference via Dice), and (iv) Slice+Roulette (discretization + inference via Roulette). The timeout is 120 seconds. 

Results. Discretization remains inexpensive across these benchmark families, and the resulting Slice+Dice/Roulette pipelines scale well with increasing program size. In contrast, exact infer-ence with Sppl exhibits substantially steeper runtime growth and reaches the timeout on several benchmark variants.

Tables 5 and 6 report the decision tree benchmarks for varying tree sizes 𝑛 . The first table contains the original decision tree benchmarks from [1], while the second extends this evaluation with additional, larger instances that we generated to more systematically study scaling as the tree size increases.

G.3 Baselines 

We finally evaluate Slice on benchmarks drawn from prior work [21, 40]. Most benchmark pro-grams are taken directly from the Sppl Github repository and translated into Slice. In some cases, the Sppl versions differ from the original Psi benchmarks, for example by replacing continuous choices with finite ones so that the programs fall within the class supported by Sppl. CoinBias and SurveyUnbias are not included in the Sppl repository, so we adapted these benchmarks in the same style. Consequently, the Slice and Sppl columns in Table 7 evaluate the same benchmark programs and provide an apples-to-apples comparison. The full benchmark programs are provided in §G.3.1. The first column in the table gives the benchmark name, the second shows the number of datasets on which to condition the program, and the remaining columns display execution times in seconds. For benchmarks with both modified and unmodified versions, the Psi and Hakaru cells report the modified timing marked by • above the unmodified timing marked by ◦. The • entries therefore provide the apples-to-apples comparison with Slice and Sppl. For Hakaru, we report disintegration, subsequent simplification, and their total. t.o. denotes the same 120s timeout used elsewhere, and err. denotes a tool error. 

Table 7. Execution times (in seconds) for benchmarks from PSI [21] and SPPL [40], with modified and unmodified Psi and Hakaru exact inference baselines. Timeout=120s. For benchmarks with two outputs for 

Psi and Hakaru, • marks the modified program and ◦ marks the unmodified program. err. represents a crash, caused by out of memory.

Results. Across the modified baseline programs in Table 7, the Slice pipelines generally provide the fastest successful end-to-end inference: Slice+Dice performs best on most benchmarks, while Slice+Roulette is faster on some instances. Sppl is generally slower, and Psi frequently times out, although both Psi and Hakaru perform better on some unmodified programs. Hakaru’s disintegra-tion phase is usually fast, but subsequent Maple simplification often times out or returns an error, limiting its end-to-end reliability.
